<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RWcasHBBErTK2FgfTFLzgn`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. This has implications beyond ThreadGate: any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing---whether per-head gating \citep{qiu2025gated}, split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring}, or residual scaling \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}---should face the same exposure bias barrier when targeting distributional properties of the attention pattern rather than the prediction itself.
4	
5	\paragraph{Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not.} NTP self-recovers because the task---predict one token from a prefix---is identical at training and inference; exposure bias shifts the prefix distribution but preserves the prediction task \citep{he2019exposure}. GAR optimizes a \emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart: the model receives no feedback on attention distributions during generation, so the training signal is specific to teacher-forced prefixes.
6	
7	\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\beta$\,=\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\% to 87.0\% (+11.1\,pp, McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077; GAR correlation $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\,pp), while format constraints show zero change---consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).
8	
9	\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} Scheduled sampling provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost ($\beta$\,=\,0); steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At high $\beta$, steering dominates and the two converge---they are substitutes, not complements ($\beta$\,=\,5: SS GAR\,=\,$0.717\pm0.001$ vs.\ TG GAR\,=\,0.727; Figure~\ref{fig:headline}b). The practical choice depends on the acceptable inference-time overhead: SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.
```

</details>

At high $\\beta$ the two converge---they are substitutes, not complements ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: SS GAR\\,=\\,$0.717\\pm0.001$ vs.\\ TG\\,=\\,0.727; Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}b). SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. This has implications beyond ThreadGate: any auxiliary attention objective paired with teacher forcing---whether per-head gating \\citep{qiu2025gated}, split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring}, or residual scaling \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}---should face the same exposure bias barrier when targeting distributional properties of the attention pattern rather than the prediction itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Why NTP self-recovers but GAR does not.} NTP self-recovers because the task---predict one token from a prefix---is identical at training and inference; exposure bias shifts the prefix distribution but preserves the prediction task \\citep{he2019exposure}. GAR optimizes a \\emph{global property of the prefix}---attention allocation to goal tokens---that has no autoregressive counterpart: the model receives no feedback on attention distributions during generation, so the training signal is specific to teacher-forced prefixes.\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream effects.} Instruction retention ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) shows late-turn compliance rising from 75.9\\% to 87.0\\% (+11.1\\,pp, McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077; GAR correlation $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007). Content constraints benefit most (+23.8\\,pp), while format constraints show zero change---consistent with GAR targeting cross-turn semantic coherence rather than locally-verifiable procedural rules (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).\n\n\\paragraph{SS and steering as substitutes.} Scheduled sampling provides training-time mitigation with no inference cost ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0); steering provides inference-time recovery with PPL cost. At high $\\beta$, steering dominates and the two converge---they are substitutes, not complements ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: SS GAR\\,=\\,$0.717\\pm0.001$ vs.\\ TG GAR\\,=\\,0.727; Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}b). The practical choice depends on the acceptable inference-time overhead: SS is preferable when inference budgets are tight, steering when retraining is infeasible.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01821EHaBkpAma5T6nMmXpKu`</summary>

```text
275	\section{Scheduled Sampling Implementation}\label{sec:appendix-ss}
276	
277	Our scheduled sampling implementation follows \citet{bengio2015scheduled} with modifications for multi-turn transformer training:
278	
279	\paragraph{Sampling schedule.} The model-generated context ratio $\rho$ increases linearly from 0 to 0.3 over 3 epochs. At each training step, a Bernoulli trial with probability $\rho$ determines whether the input prefix is replaced with model-generated tokens. The effective usage rate (29.7--37.1\%) is lower than the nominal schedule due to per-sample Bernoulli variance.
280	
281	\paragraph{Context generation.} When model-generated context is selected, the model autoregressively generates the assistant turns from the conversation prefix up to the current training position. The generated tokens replace the ground-truth assistant tokens in the input, while user turns remain unchanged. This preserves the multi-turn structure while introducing distributional diversity in the conditioning context.
282	
283	\paragraph{Training dynamics.} The scheduled sampling variant converges to similar final loss (NTP 0.932 vs.\ 0.930 for standard ThreadGate) but with higher variance in early epochs due to the stochastic context mixing. Gate profiles remain similar (mean 0.953 vs.\ 0.947, min at L4 vs.\ L1), indicating that scheduled sampling does not fundamentally alter gate optimization but rather improves the robustness of the learned attention patterns to distributional shift. Training time increases by 8--80\% across seeds (seed-dependent; dominated by GAR forward pass overhead): 6.5h (seed 42) and 10.8h (seed 123) vs.\ 6.0h for standard ThreadGate on Qwen.
284	
285	\section{Computational Cost Analysis}\label{sec:appendix-cost}
286	
287	\begin{table}[h]
288	\centering\small
289	\caption{Computational cost across training configurations. All experiments use a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (102\,GB VRAM).}
290	\label{tab:cost}
291	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
292	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
293	\toprule
294	\textbf{Config} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Time (h)} & \textbf{VRAM} & \textbf{Overhead} \\
295	\midrule
296	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct}} \\
297	LoRA-only & 4.2M & 6.0 & 42\,GB & baseline \\
298	ThreadGate & 4.3M & 6.0 & 43\,GB & $+$57K \\
299	\;+\,PCGrad & 4.3M & 8.0 & 43\,GB & $+$33\% \\
300	Uniform Gate & 4.3M & 6.0 & 43\,GB & $+$57K \\
301	SS (s42) & 4.3M & 6.5 & 43\,GB & $+$8\% \\
302	SS (s123) & 4.3M & 10.8 & 43\,GB & $+$80\% \\
303	\midrule
304	\multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct}} \\
305	LoRA-only & 4.2M & 7.0 & 45\,GB & baseline \\
306	ThreadGate & 4.3M & 7.3 & 46\,GB & $+$65K \\
307	\bottomrule
308	\end{tabular}}
309	\end{table}
```

The generated tokens replace the ground-truth assistant tokens in the input, while user turns remain unchanged. This preserves the multi-turn structure while introducing distributional diversity in the conditioning context.\n\n\\paragraph{Training dynamics.} The scheduled sampling variant converges to similar final loss (NTP 0.932 vs.\\ 0.930 for standard ThreadGate) but with higher variance in early epochs due to the stochastic context mixing. Gate profiles remain similar (mean 0.953 vs.\\ 0.947, min at L4 vs.\\ L1), indicating that scheduled sampling does not fundamentally alter gate optimization but rather improves the robustness of the learned attention patterns to distributional shift. Training time increases by 8--80\\% across seeds (seed-dependent; dominated by GAR forward pass overhead): 6.5h (seed 42) and 10.8h (seed 123) vs.\\ 6.0h for standard ThreadGate on Qwen.\n\n\\section{Computational Cost Analysis}\\label{sec:appendix-cost}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\\small\n\\caption{Computational cost across training configurations. All experiments use a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (102\\,GB VRAM).}\n\\label{tab:cost}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Time (h)} & \\textbf{VRAM} & \\textbf{Overhead} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\emph{Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct}} \\\\\nLoRA-only & 4.2M & 6.0 & 42\\,GB & baseline \\\\\nThreadGate & 4.3M & 6.0 & 43\\,GB & $+$57K \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad & 4.3M & 8.0 & 43\\,GB & $+$33\\% \\\\\nUniform Gate & 4.3M & 6.0 & 43\\,GB & $+$57K \\\\\nSS (s42) & 4.3M & 6.5 & 43\\,GB & $+$8\\% \\\\\nSS (s123) & 4.3M & 10.8 & 43\\,GB & $+$80\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{l}{\\emph{Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct}} \\\\\nLoRA-only & 4.2M & 7.0 & 45\\,GB & baseline \\\\\nThreadGate & 4.3M & 7.3 & 46\\,GB & $+$65K \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}",
      "numLines": 35,

    }

_source line 3178 · 2026-05-24T03:17:25.445Z_

[Director 指令] [Patch] appendix.tex KL section 排名描述修正

Reviewer 发现 appendix.tex L387 的模型排名与 Table 1 AR 数据不符。

**当前错误文字**: "ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling retain the highest mid-turn GAR, followed by PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, and Base (in decreasing order)"

**实际 AR GAR 排名（Table 1 数据）**:
SS (0.401) >>> TG (0.064) >> LoRA-only (0.025) > Base (0.019) > PCGrad (0.015) ≈ UG (0.015)

**两个问题**:
1. SS 和 TG 不应并列——SS 是 TG 的 6.3 倍
2. PCGrad/UG/LO/Base 排名顺序错误

**修正要求**:
- 交叉核对 fig_kl_ar_gar_bar.pdf 中的实际条形图顺序
- 如果 CPU proxy 分析用的是不同指标（如 per-turn trajectory 而非 Table 1 的 mid-turn mean），在文中注明数据来源差异
- 否则修正排名描述以匹配 Table 1

这次不用验证引用。

370	\end{table}
371	
372	\section{Exposure Bias Scaling}\label{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}
373	
374	Figure~\ref{fig:eb-scaling} quantifies two dimensions of the exposure bias phenomenon. Panel~(a) compares the TF/AR gap ratio across all training variants and both architectures. On Qwen, ThreadGate achieves the smallest gap among standard variants (7.8$\times$) while PCGrad shows the largest (16.7$\times$, consistent with its counterproductive effect on AR GAR). Scheduled sampling reduces the gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$, the only variant approaching parity. On Llama, both ThreadGate and LoRA-only show $\sim$27$\times$ gaps (by median), larger than any Qwen variant. Panel~(b) shows that the gap compounds with conversation length: at 3.6 mean turns ($N$\,=\,50), ThreadGate's gap is 8$\times$; at 10 turns ($N$\,=\,5), it widens to 31$\times$. This length dependence is consistent with cumulative distributional drift---each model-generated turn introduces additional divergence from the teacher-forced training distribution, and by turn~10 the accumulated drift overwhelms any gating signal.
375	
376	\begin{figure*}[h]
377	\centering
378	\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{fig_eb_scaling.pdf}
379	\caption{Exposure bias scaling analysis. (a)~TF/AR gap ratio across training variants; scheduled sampling (SS, ${\sim}$1.3$\times$) is the only variant near parity. Llama gaps ($\sim$27$\times$) exceed all Qwen variants. (b)~Gap compounds with conversation length: ThreadGate's gap widens from 8$\times$ (3.6 turns) to 31$\times$ (10 turns).}
380	\label{fig:eb-scaling}
381	\end{figure*}
382	
383	\section{Per-Turn AR GAR Dynamics}\label{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}
384	
385	To characterize how goal attention evolves across conversation turns under autoregressive generation, we compute per-turn AR GAR for all six Qwen-2.5-7B training configurations (Base, LoRA-only, Uniform Gate, PCGrad, Scheduled Sampling, ThreadGate) on the held-out evaluation set. Figure~\ref{fig:kl_ar_gar_curves} plots per-turn AR GAR trajectories across turns 1--5, and Figure~\ref{fig:kl_ar_gar_bar} compares mid-turn AR GAR levels across configurations.
386	
387	All six configurations exhibit monotonic GAR decay as turn index increases, consistent with cumulative exposure bias eroding attention to goal tokens. The decay rate, however, varies substantially across training strategies. ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling retain the highest mid-turn GAR, followed by PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, and Base (in decreasing order). Base and LoRA-only decay most rapidly, losing the majority of their initial GAR within the first few turns. ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling maintain comparatively higher GAR through turn 5, suggesting that both turn-conditioned gating and distributional robustification provide partial protection against attention drift.
388	
389	\begin{figure}[t]
390	\centering
391	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig_kl_ar_gar_curves.pdf}
392	\caption{Per-turn AR GAR trajectories for six Qwen-2.5-7B configurations (turns 1--5). All models show monotonic decay; ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling decay slowest, while Base and LoRA-only decay fastest.}
393	\label{fig:kl_ar_gar_curves}
394	\end{figure}
395	
396	\begin{figure}[t]
397	\centering
398	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig_kl_ar_gar_bar.pdf}
399	\caption{Mid-turn AR GAR comparison across six training configurations. Ranking from highest to lowest: ThreadGate, Scheduled Sampling, PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base.}

The decay rate, however, varies substantially across training strategies. Base and LoRA-only decay most rapidly, losing the majority of their initial GAR within the first few turns. ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling maintain comparatively higher GAR through turn 5, suggesting that both turn-conditioned gating and distributional robustification provide partial protection against attention drift.",
  "new_string": "All six configurations exhibit monotonic GAR decay as turn index increases, consistent with cumulative exposure bias eroding attention to goal tokens. The decay rate varies substantially: Scheduled Sampling retains by far the highest mid-turn GAR (0.423), followed distantly by ThreadGate (0.064), LoRA-only (0.037), Uniform Gate (0.033), PCGrad (0.030), and Base (0.019). Base decays most rapidly, losing the majority of its initial GAR within the first few turns. The large SS--TG gap mirrors Table~\\ref{tab:four-way} and confirms that distributional robustification---not gating architecture alone---is the primary driver of AR GAR retention."
}

_source line 3202 · 2026-05-24T03:18:32.614Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The curves caption says "ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling decay slowest" — looking at the data:
- SS: 0.526 → 0.391 (drops 0.135, retains ~74%)
- TG: 0.130 → 0.066 (drops 0.064, retains ~51%)
- LoRA: 0.107 → 0.040 (drops 0.067, retains ~37%)
- Base: 0.085 → 0.004 (drops 0.081, retains ~5%)

SS does decay slowest in absolute terms and retains the most. 

But grouping ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling together is misleading since SS maintains 4-6× higher values throughout the decay curve. While TG does retain more than the others proportionally, the caption should reflect that SS is the clear winner here, with TG as a distant second.
```

</details>

          "-\\caption{Per-turn AR GAR trajectories for six Qwen-2.5-7B configurations (turns 1--5). All models show monotonic decay; ThreadGate and Scheduled Sampling decay slowest, while Base and LoRA-only decay fastest.}",

          " \\label{fig:kl_ar_gar_curves}",
          " \\end{figure}",
          " "
        ]
      }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WMa1Fqy9uQ5r7n6g3crHfW`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	The five-way comparison (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \S\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.
4	
5	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
6	
7	We compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\% attenuation; Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\,=\,0.777, L27\,=\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	  \centering
11	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth,height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
12	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\,=\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}
13	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
17	
18	NTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\times$ to 14.7$\times$. If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.
19	
20	PCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \emph{worsens}: 0.064$\to$0.030 ($p$\,=\,0.002; Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.
21	
22	\begin{figure}[t]
23	  \centering
24	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth,height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}
25	  \caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}
26	  \label{fig:training-pcgrad}
27	\end{figure}
28	
29	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
30	
31	The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\% per-turn decay; Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}).
32	
33	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
34	
35	We test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\times$) vs.\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.
36	
37	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
38	
39	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\,=\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\,$>$\,100\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\,$\geq$\,0.4, std\,=\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\% spikes (std\,=\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\times$ and 17$\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).
40	
41	Gate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\,=\,0.793, L5\,=\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\,=\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates. The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\times$ on Qwen, 27$\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	  \centering
45	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}
46	  \caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}
47	  \label{fig:crossmodel}
48	\end{figure}
```

      "content": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$. If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).\n\nGate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793, L5\\,=\\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	
6	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\,GB). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; the controlled comparison design is described in \S\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.
7	
8	\subsection{Main Results}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\leq$2\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\leq$1\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, confirming that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\S\ref{subsec:ss}).
11	
12	Under AR generation ($N$\,=\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}a). The ordering---ThreadGate (0.064) $>$ LoRA-only (0.037) $>$ Uniform Gate (0.033) $>$ PCGrad (0.030) $>$ Base (0.019)---shows ThreadGate vs.\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\,=\,0.004; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\pm0.001$). We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
13	
14	\begin{table*}[t]
15	\centering
16	\small
17	\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. TF evaluation uses IID UltraChat and 50 held-out conversations; AR evaluation uses $N$\,=\,50 held-out conversations (mean 3.6 user turns). All fine-tuned variants converge under TF ($\leq$2\,pp spread); the 8--17$\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all configurations. $\bar{g}\!\pm\!\sigma_g$: learned gate mean and std across 28 layers (LoRA-only has no gates). GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}.}
18	\label{tab:four-way}
19	\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc cc c}
20	\toprule
21	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{AR ($N$\,=\,50)}} & \\
22	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7} \cmidrule(lr){8-9}
23	\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & 95\% CI & $\bar{g}\!\pm\!\sigma_g$ \\
24	\midrule
25	\multicolumn{10}{l}{\emph{Baseline}} \\
26	Base              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.019 & {\scriptsize[.014,\,.024]} & --- \\
27	\midrule
28	\multicolumn{10}{l}{\emph{Gated variants}} \\
29	ThreadGate        & 0.50 & \textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & 0.50 & \textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & \textbf{0.064} & {\scriptsize[.041,\,.091]} & .93\,{\scriptsize$\pm$\,.05} \\
30	\;+\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.030 & {\scriptsize[.023,\,.038]} & --- \\
31	Uniform Gate      & 0.49 & 0.01 & 2.78 & \textbf{0.51} & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.033 & {\scriptsize[.025,\,.043]} & .96\,{\scriptsize$\pm$\,.05} \\
32	\midrule
33	\multicolumn{10}{l}{\emph{Ungated}} \\
34	LoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.037 & {\scriptsize[.027,\,.050]} & 1.00 \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}
37	\end{table*}
38	
39	\begin{figure}[t]
40	  \centering
41	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
42	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\,=\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\% decay; $\Delta$\,=\,3.3\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect on the attention decay pattern.}
43	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
44	\end{figure}
45	
46	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
47	
48	All training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:
49	\begin{equation}
50	  \tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} + \beta \cdot \mathbf{1}[j \in \mathcal{G}],
51	\end{equation}
52	where $\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}b). The response is sharply nonlinear: $\beta \in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\,=\,0.556 (9\% PPL cost). At $\beta$\,=\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\beta \in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-steering}).
53	
54	This closes the causal loop: if the collapse were due to representation drift in hidden states, no amount of attention reweighting could recover GAR. The recovery confirms attention misallocation---not representation degradation---as the proximal cause.
55	
56	\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\label{subsec:ss}
57	
58	Scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}---mixing model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability---directly targets the training-inference mismatch identified in \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift}. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises from 0.064 to $0.401\pm0.047$ (seeds: 0.347 / 0.423 / 0.433), compressing the gap from 7.8$\times$ to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ with no PPL cost ($1.441\pm0.001$).\footnote{Seed 123 TF GAR unavailable (checkpoint error); per-seed gaps: 1.45$\times$ (s42), 1.15$\times$ (s456).} Steering on SS models is remarkably stable ($\beta$\,=\,5: $0.717\pm0.001$): the training-time seed variance (std\,=\,0.047 at $\beta$\,=\,0) is nearly eliminated, suggesting $\beta$-steering converges to a seed-independent attention equilibrium. GAR-Dongre also rises substantially under SS ($N$\,=\,5 pilot; see Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-n5})---the only intervention that moves both metrics. The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.
59	
60

We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.",
    "newString": "each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-data}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \\citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\\leq$1\\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, confirming that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{subsec:ss}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}a). The ordering---ThreadGate (0.064) $>$ LoRA-only (0.037) $>$ Uniform Gate (0.033) $>$ PCGrad (0.030) $>$ Base (0.019)---shows ThreadGate vs.\\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\\,=\\,0.004; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. TF evaluation uses IID UltraChat and 50 held-out conversations; AR evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,50 held-out conversations (mean 3.6 user turns). All fine-tuned variants converge under TF ($\\leq$2\\,pp spread); the 8--17$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all configurations. $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$: learned gate mean and std across 28 layers (LoRA-only has no gates). GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{AR ($N$\\,=\\,50)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & 95\\% CI & $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Baseline}} \\\\\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.019 & {\\scriptsize[.014,\\,.024]} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Gated variants}} \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.064} & {\\scriptsize[.041,\\,.091]} & .93\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.030 & {\\scriptsize[.023,\\,.038]} & --- \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.49 & 0.01 & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.51} & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.033 & {\\scriptsize[.025,\\,.043]} & .96\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Ungated}} \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.037 & {\\scriptsize[.027,\\,.050]} & 1.00 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\\% decay; $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.3\\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect on the attention decay pattern.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}b). The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}).\n\nThis closes the causal loop: if the collapse were due to representation drift in hidden states, no amount of attention reweighting could recover GAR. The recovery confirms attention misallocation---not representation degradation---as the proximal cause.\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\nScheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}---mixing model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability---directly targets the training-inference mismatch identified in \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises from 0.064 to $0.401\\pm0.047$ (seeds: 0.347 / 0.423 / 0.433), compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$).\\footnote{Seed 123 TF GAR unavailable (checkpoint error); per-seed gaps: 1.45$\\times$ (s42), 1.15$\\times$ (s456).} Steering on SS models is remarkably stable ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$): the training-time seed variance (std\\,=\\,0.047 at $\\beta$\\,=\\,0) is nearly eliminated, suggesting $\\beta$-steering converges to a seed-independent attention equilibrium. GAR-Dongre also rises substantially under SS ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot; see Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5})---the only intervention that moves both metrics. The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$. If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\\,$\\geq$\\,0.4, std\\,=\\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\\% spikes (std\\,=\\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).\n\nGate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793, L5\\,=\\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates. The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}\n  \\label{fig:crossmodel}\n\\end{figure}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\\,$\\geq$\\,0.4, std\\,=\\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\\% spikes (std\\,=\\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).\n\nGate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793, L5\\,=\\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates. The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}\n  \\label{fig:crossmodel}\n\\end{figure}",

    "newString": "Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\\,$\\geq$\\,0.4, std\\,=\\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\\% spikes (std\\,=\\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).\n\nGate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793, L5\\,=\\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates. The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}\n  \\label{fig:crossmodel}\n\\end{figure}",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VkicTzSKuV4qyCbTNtxQYJ`</summary>

```text
37	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
38	
39	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\,=\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\,$>$\,100\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\,$\geq$\,0.4, std\,=\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\% spikes (std\,=\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\times$ and 17$\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).
40	
41	Gate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\,=\,0.793, L5\,=\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\,=\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates (additional visualizations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\times$ on Qwen, 27$\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	  \centering
45	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}
46	  \caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}
47	  \label{fig:crossmodel}
48	\end{figure}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe five-way comparison (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) yields a paradox: training succeeds but inference fails, and no single architectural factor explains the gap. This section resolves it through a causal exclusion chain---each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning} kills ``turn conditioning rescues late-turn attention''; \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad} kills ``gradient conflict prevents GAR convergence''; \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} identifies exposure bias as the surviving explanation. \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity via an orthogonal metric, and \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms generalization across architectures.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$)---e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning, confirming the structure arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base).}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}---projecting conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane---should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the cause. The 7.5--14.7$\\times$ magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth,height=0.42\\textheight,keepaspectratio]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID GAR and PPL are unchanged; AR GAR worsens (0.064$\\to$0.030), ruling out gradient conflict as the cause.}\n  \\label{fig:training-pcgrad}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention while leaving diffuse attention unaffected. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. NTP self-recovers \\citep{he2019exposure} because prediction is the same task at both regimes; GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart. Attention patterns over model-generated tokens have no training-time analogue, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}; per-turn dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed), confirming exposure bias disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\\,$\\geq$\\,0.4, std\\,=\\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\\% spikes (std\\,=\\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\\times$ and 17$\\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).\n\nGate profiles differ qualitatively across architectures (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793, L5\\,=\\,0.797) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at output-adjacent layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), suggesting that the location of maximum gate attenuation is architecture-dependent---early-middle for Qwen, late-middle for Llama---but the convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal. Both architectures share the same outcome: gates learn per-layer variation during training but are driven toward passivity at inference, where NTP dominates (additional visualizations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\\times$ on Qwen, 27$\\times$ (median) on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_crossmodel.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cross-architecture validation. (a)~The TF-AR gap persists on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ by median), exceeding Qwen's 8$\\times$. (b)~Gate profiles on normalized depth: Qwen dips at early layers, Llama at late layers---architecture-dependent patterns, but both converge toward identity.}\n  \\label{fig:crossmodel}\n\\end{figure}",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VU4p4CA2XvwL3ouS6eqkzS`</summary>

```text
37	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
38	
39	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\,=\,20 conversations, 4 turns each). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) and 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit, consistent with Qwen. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (CoV\,$>$\,100\%): ThreadGate shows a 5\% spike ratio (2/40 turns with GAR\,$\geq$\,0.4, std\,=\,0.100) while LoRA-only has 0\% spikes (std\,=\,0.048). We therefore report medians: 0.018 for both TG and LoRA-only (Base: 0.009), yielding TF-AR gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}a). Mean GAR-ours is 0.043 (TG) and 0.028 (LoRA-only), gaps of 11$\times$ and 17$\times$---the mean-median divergence confirms that rare high-GAR turns inflate means without reflecting typical model behavior (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}).
40	
41	Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\,=\,0.793) while Llama shows a V-shaped valley at late layers (L25\,=\,0.719)---architecture-dependent locations, but convergence toward identity under NTP pressure is universal (additional visualizations in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures (8--17$\times$ on Qwen, 27$\times$ on Llama), consistent with exposure bias as an architecture-independent phenomenon.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	  \centering
```
